Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I've been struggling with overwhelming, intrusive thoughts and worries that I just can't seem to shake. Specifically, I have this intense fear of germs and contamination, and it is starting to completely take over my daily life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific repetitive actions, such as excessive hand washing or cleaning, to try and relieve the anxiety caused by these thoughts?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about doing those kinds of specific actions to relieve the anxiety, as I mostly just get completely stuck in my head with the worrying.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries primarily focused on the fear of contamination, or do you find yourself constantly worrying about a wide variety of everyday topics, such as your job, health, finances, or family safety?\n\nPatient: My worries are very specifically focused on contamination, germs, and dirt. I don't really spend time worrying about everyday things like my job, finances, or general life stressors.\n\nDoctor: Do you recognize these thoughts about contamination as being irrational or excessive, even though you find them difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't thought about whether they are irrational or not. They just feel incredibly real in the moment, and they are so difficult to ignore.\n\nDoctor: How much time each day would you say you spend caught up in these intrusive thoughts about contamination, and does this significantly interfere with your ability to work or socialize?\n\nPatient: I'd say I spend several hours every single day consumed by these thoughts about contamination and germs. It absolutely interferes with my life, making it really hard to focus on my work or even go out to socialize with my friends.\n\nDoctor: Since you don't perform physical actions like cleaning, do you find yourself performing 'mental rituals'—such as repeating specific words, counting, or mentally reviewing events—to try and neutralize these distressing thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really perform those kinds of mental rituals like counting or repeating words. I mostly just get stuck feeling incredibly distressed and anxious by the thoughts themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical symptoms of anxiety—such as muscle tension, restlessness, being easily fatigued, or difficulty concentrating—on most days when you are caught in these cycles of worry?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed or experienced physical symptoms like muscle tension or fatigue. My main struggle is definitely the intense mental distress and anxiety that comes directly from these obsessive thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to identify any specific 'triggers' in your environment, such as touching doorknobs or public surfaces, that reliably cause these thoughts to spike?\n\nPatient: Yes, touching public surfaces, doorknobs, or anything that other people have handled is a huge trigger for me. The moment I make contact with those things, it immediately sparks intense, intrusive worries about germs, dirt, and contamination. It is incredibly difficult to shake those thoughts once they start.\n\nDoctor: Because of these fears, do you find yourself actively avoiding certain places or situations, such as public restrooms or public transportation, to prevent coming into contact with these triggers?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, and I haven't really thought about whether I actively avoid those specific places. Mostly, I just find myself constantly overwhelmed by the intense worry about germs and contamination whenever I am out in public.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts about contamination arise, do they feel like they are coming from your own mind, or do they feel like intrusive, unwanted 'intruders' that you cannot stop from entering your thoughts?\n\nPatient: They definitely feel like intrusive, unwanted intruders that I absolutely cannot stop from entering my mind. It feels like my thoughts are being hijacked, which causes me an immense amount of distress. I just have no control over them once they start."

Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
